Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I’m trying to listen, honestly, but sometimes people give me these looks like I’m rushing them or impatient, and that’s not really what I mean to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I don’t mean to be rude, but I’ve noticed I’ll jump in before someone’s finished. Like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, I’ll cut in, or at work I might finish an employee’s thought before they’ve actually said it. I usually think I’m helping or moving things along, but… I can tell it rubs people the wrong way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing group activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that as a big problem, honestly. Lines can annoy me like anyone else, but I don’t think I’m cutting ahead or making a scene or anything. In group stuff, I try to wait my turn… I mean, maybe I get a little eager sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects due to disorganization or difficulty with planning?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a major problem. I mean, I run a company, so I have to plan things and get things finished. Sometimes I probably rely on other people or reminders to keep things moving, but… I don’t think I’m dropping the ball all the time or anything. Maybe if you mean personal projects at home, those can kind of sit around longer than they should, but I’ve always figured that’s pretty normal. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always, but it does happen. Like I can start a planning document or sit down with one of my managers and feel pretty locked in at first, and then after a few minutes I realize I’ve kind of missed half of what they said. Then I have to ask them to run it back, which is a little embarrassing. I try to cover it well, but yeah, focusing for a long stretch can be harder than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably do that more than I’d like to admit. I don’t usually mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll jump in before someone’s finished, like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, or at work I’ll finish an employee’s thought because I think I know where they’re going. Sometimes I can tell afterward it didn’t land well. I try to catch myself, but… yeah, it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself quitting tasks or activities before you've finished them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I quit things a lot. I mean, if something matters, I usually get it done, especially at work. There are probably things I start and then set aside for a while, but I don’t know if that’s “quitting” exactly. Maybe more like I move on to something else and come back later… or sometimes don’t, if it wasn’t that important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m barging in all the time, but I have noticed I’ll cut people off more than I should.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I guess what finally made me come in is that over the past year or so, I’ve been getting pulled off task really easily. It’s not anything dramatic, I don’t think, but even a phone buzz, someone walking past my office, or just some random thought—like about my in-laws—can derail what I’m doing before I really catch it. I figured I should probably ask someone if that’s normal or if I’m just… not managing it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s out of control or anything, but I do notice it. In meetings especially, I’m usually bouncing my knee or clicking a pen, or I’ll keep shifting around in the chair. I’m trying to listen, honestly, but sometimes people give me these looks like I’m rushing them or impatient, and that’s not really what I mean to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I don’t mean to be rude, but I’ve noticed I’ll jump in before someone’s finished. Like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, I’ll cut in, or at work I might finish an employee’s thought before they’ve actually said it. I usually think I’m helping or moving things along, but… I can tell it rubs people the wrong way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing group activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that as a big problem, honestly. Lines can annoy me like anyone else, but I don’t think I’m cutting ahead or making a scene or anything. In group stuff, I try to wait my turn… I mean, maybe I get a little eager sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects due to disorganization or difficulty with planning?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a major problem. I mean, I run a company, so I have to plan things and get things finished. Sometimes I probably rely on other people or reminders to keep things moving, but… I don’t think I’m dropping the ball all the time or anything. Maybe if you mean personal projects at home, those can kind of sit around longer than they should, but I’ve always figured that’s pretty normal. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always, but it does happen. Then I have to ask them to run it back, which is a little embarrassing. I try to cover it well, but yeah, focusing for a long stretch can be harder than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably do that more than I’d like to admit. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others' conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. I mean… I’m still keeping up overall. I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart, because it’s not. I do my job, I show up for my family.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I guess what finally made me come in is that over the past year or so, I’ve been getting pulled off task really easily. It’s not anything dramatic, I don’t think, but even a phone buzz, someone walking past my office, or just some random thought—like about my in-laws—can derail what I’m doing before I really catch it. I figured I should probably ask someone if that’s normal or if I’m just… not managing it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s out of control or anything, but I do notice it. In meetings especially, I’m usually bouncing my knee or clicking a pen, or I’ll keep shifting around in the chair. I’m trying to listen, honestly, but sometimes people give me these looks like I’m rushing them or impatient, and that’s not really what I mean to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I don’t mean to be rude, but I’ve noticed I’ll jump in before someone’s finished. Like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, I’ll cut in, or at work I might finish an employee’s thought before they’ve actually said it. I usually think I’m helping or moving things along, but… I can tell it rubs people the wrong way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing group activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that as a big problem, honestly. Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to dominate the room or anything. But I do catch myself talking a lot more than I meant to.

Yeah… I think so, to some degree. I’m still functioning, you know? I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m getting through work, I’m taking care of things at home.

But I do catch myself talking a lot more than I meant to.\n\nEspecially at work, if I’m explaining a plan or walking someone through what I want done, I’ll get going and sort of keep adding details. I wouldn’t say it’s constant, but it happens enough that I’m aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, to some degree. When family plans don’t go the way they should, I can feel that trust at home take a hit. Yeah… I think so. I mean, I was a kid, so it’s hard to know what was “normal,” but looking back, I was probably more restless than some of the other kids.

I don’t mean to be rude, but I’ve noticed I’ll jump in before someone’s finished. I don’t mean to be rude, but I’ve noticed I’ll jump in before someone’s finished. I usually think I’m helping or moving things along, but… I can tell it rubs people the wrong way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing group activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that as a big problem, honestly. Lines can annoy me like anyone else, but I don’t think I’m cutting ahead or making a scene or anything. In group stuff, I try to wait my turn… I mean, maybe I get a little eager sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects due to disorganization or difficulty with planning?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a major problem. I mean, I run a company, so I have to plan things and get things finished. Sometimes I probably rely on other people or reminders to keep things moving, but… I don’t think I’m dropping the ball all the time or anything. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always, but it does happen. I don’t usually mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll jump in before someone’s finished, like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, or at work I’ll finish an employee’s thought because I think I know where they’re going. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're 'on the go' or like you're constantly in motion, even when you're not necessarily supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a dramatic way, I don’t think. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I guess what finally made me come in is that over the past year or so, I’ve been getting pulled off task really easily. It’s not anything dramatic, I don’t think, but even a phone buzz, someone walking past my office, or just some random thought—like about my in-laws—can derail what I’m doing before I really catch it. I figured I should probably ask someone if that’s normal or if I’m just… not managing it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s out of control or anything, but I do notice it. In meetings especially, I’m usually bouncing my knee or clicking a pen, or I’ll keep shifting around in the chair. I’m trying to listen, honestly, but sometimes people give me these looks like I’m rushing them or impatient, and that’s not really what I mean to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I don’t mean to be rude, but I’ve noticed I’ll jump in before someone’s finished. Like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, I’ll cut in, or at work I might finish an employee’s thought before they’ve actually said it. I usually think I’m helping or moving things along, but… I can tell it rubs people the wrong way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing group activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that as a big problem, honestly. Lines can annoy me like anyone else, but I don’t think I’m cutting ahead or making a scene or anything. In group stuff, I try to wait my turn… I mean, maybe I get a little eager sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects due to disorganization or difficulty with planning?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a major problem. I mean, I run a company, so I have to plan things and get things finished. Sometimes I probably rely on other people or reminders to keep things moving, but… I don’t think I’m dropping the ball all the time or anything. Maybe if you mean personal projects at home, those can kind of sit around longer than they should, but I’ve always figured that’s pretty normal. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always, but it does happen. Like I can start a planning document or sit down with one of my managers and feel pretty locked in at first, and then after a few minutes I realize I’ve kind of missed half of what they said. Then I have to ask them to run it back, which is a little embarrassing. I try to cover it well, but yeah, focusing for a long stretch can be harder than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably do that more than I’d like to admit. I don’t usually mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll jump in before someone’s finished, like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, or at work I’ll finish an employee’s thought because I think I know where they’re going. Sometimes I can tell afterward it didn’t land well. I try to catch myself, but… yeah, it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself quitting tasks or activities before you've finished them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I quit things a lot. I mean, if something matters, I usually get it done, especially at work. There are probably things I start and then set aside for a while, but I don’t know if that’s “quitting” exactly. Maybe more like I move on to something else and come back later… or sometimes don’t, if it wasn’t that important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're 'on the go' or like you're constantly in motion, even when you're not necessarily supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a dramatic way, I don’t think. But when I’m supposed to just sit still, like at a family dinner or one of the church volunteer meetings, I can feel this sort of… motor running inside. Like I need to move, or tap my foot, or do something with my hands.\n\nI try not to make it obvious, because I know it can look impatient or rude, but yeah, I do feel kind of constantly wound up sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others' conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m barging in all the time, but I have noticed I’ll cut people off more than I should.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s talking with her parents, I might jump in before I really should. Or at work, if an employee is explaining something, I’ll kind of finish their thought because I think I know where they’re headed. I usually mean it to be helpful, but… I can tell sometimes it rubs people the wrong way. So, yeah, I probably do intrude more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still keeping up overall. I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart, because it’s not. I do my job, I show up for my family.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, it does cause some problems. At home, I think it’s starting to cost me a little trust when plans don’t go the way they were supposed to. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, people can get kind of worn down or less patient with me if I jump ahead or don’t handle the conversation the way I should.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is affecting things. Not severely, maybe, but enough that I’m noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, to the point where others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to dominate the room or anything. But I do catch myself talking a lot more than I meant to.\n\nEspecially at work, if I’m explaining a plan or walking someone through what I want done, I’ll get going and sort of keep adding details. Then I notice the other person hasn’t really gotten a chance to say much. Sometimes I can see it on their face before I realize it.\n\nSo… yes, probably. I wouldn’t say it’s constant, but it happens enough that I’m aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, to some degree. I’m still functioning, you know? I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m getting through work, I’m taking care of things at home.\n\nBut it does wear on me a little. I try to cover it well, but yeah, focusing for a long stretch can be harder than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably do that more than I’d like to admit. I don’t usually mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll jump in before someone’s finished, like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, or at work I’ll finish an employee’s thought because I think I know where they’re going. Sometimes I can tell afterward it didn’t land well. I try to catch myself, but… yeah, it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself quitting tasks or activities before you've finished them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I quit things a lot. I mean, if something matters, I usually get it done, especially at work. There are probably things I start and then set aside for a while, but I don’t know if that’s “quitting” exactly. Maybe more like I move on to something else and come back later… or sometimes don’t, if it wasn’t that important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're 'on the go' or like you're constantly in motion, even when you're not necessarily supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a dramatic way, I don’t think. But when I’m supposed to just sit still, like at a family dinner or one of the church volunteer meetings, I can feel this sort of… motor running inside. Like I need to move, or tap my foot, or do something with my hands.\n\nI try not to make it obvious, because I know it can look impatient or rude, but yeah, I do feel kind of constantly wound up sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others' conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m barging in all the time, but I have noticed I’ll cut people off more than I should.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s talking with her parents, I might jump in before I really should. Or at work, if an employee is explaining something, I’ll kind of finish their thought because I think I know where they’re headed. I usually mean it to be helpful, but… I can tell sometimes it rubs people the wrong way. So, yeah, I probably do intrude more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still keeping up overall. I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart, because it’s not. I do my job, I show up for my family.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, it does cause some problems. At home, I think it’s starting to cost me a little trust when plans don’t go the way they were supposed to. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, people can get kind of worn down or less patient with me if I jump ahead or don’t handle the conversation the way I should.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is affecting things. Not severely, maybe, but enough that I’m noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, to the point where others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to dominate the room or anything. But I do catch myself talking a lot more than I meant to.\n\nEspecially at work, if I’m explaining a plan or walking someone through what I want done, I’ll get going and sort of keep adding details. Then I notice the other person hasn’t really gotten a chance to say much. Sometimes I can see it on their face before I realize it.\n\nSo… yes, probably. I wouldn’t say it’s constant, but it happens enough that I’m aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, to some degree. I’m still functioning, you know? I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m getting through work, I’m taking care of things at home.\n\nBut it does wear on me a little. When family plans don’t go the way they should, I can feel that trust at home take a hit. And at work, if meetings or mentoring conversations go sideways because of how I handle them, I can tell people get less patient with me.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it affects my quality of life some. Not in a huge dramatic way, but enough that I don’t feel great about it.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless as a child, compared to your peers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I was a kid, so it’s hard to know what was “normal,” but looking back, I was probably more restless than some of the other kids.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they’d even finished asking the question. I don’t know if that counts exactly, but… yeah, that was definitely a thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or following instructions as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t remember it being a big thing, at least not like… nobody sat me down and said I couldn’t pay attention or follow directions.\n\nI probably missed things here and there like any kid, but I don’t want to overstate it. The stuff I remember more clearly was being kind of restless or talking when I shouldn’t. With instructions, I think I usually managed okay if someone kept me on track. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and impulsivity as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, looking back, I guess there were some similar things. I don’t want to make it sound like I was out of control or anything, but teachers did move my seat around a lot because I’d talk too much, or get up when I probably wasn’t supposed to.\n\nAnd I remember blurting out answers before they’d even finished asking the question. So… more the impulsive, restless side, I think. If that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your symptoms are causing mild, moderate, or significant problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say mild… maybe edging toward moderate sometimes, but I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI’m still keeping up overall. Work is getting done, home life is still functioning. But it does cost me a bit, I guess. People at work can lose patience with me in meetings or mentoring conversations when they don’t go smoothly, and at home it can affect trust when family plans don’t happen the way they should.\n\nSo… I’d say mild, but noticeable. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Maybe if you mean personal projects at home, those can kind of sit around longer than they should, but I’ve always figured that’s pretty normal. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always, but it does happen. Like I can start a planning document or sit down with one of my managers and feel pretty locked in at first, and then after a few minutes I realize I’ve kind of missed half of what they said. Then I have to ask them to run it back, which is a little embarrassing. I try to cover it well, but yeah, focusing for a long stretch can be harder than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably do that more than I’d like to admit. I don’t usually mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll jump in before someone’s finished, like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, or at work I’ll finish an employee’s thought because I think I know where they’re going. Sometimes I can tell afterward it didn’t land well. I try to catch myself, but… yeah, it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself quitting tasks or activities before you've finished them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I quit things a lot. I mean, if something matters, I usually get it done, especially at work. There are probably things I start and then set aside for a while, but I don’t know if that’s “quitting” exactly. Maybe more like I move on to something else and come back later… or sometimes don’t, if it wasn’t that important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're 'on the go' or like you're constantly in motion, even when you're not necessarily supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a dramatic way, I don’t think. But when I’m supposed to just sit still, like at a family dinner or one of the church volunteer meetings, I can feel this sort of… motor running inside. Like I need to move, or tap my foot, or do something with my hands.\n\nI try not to make it obvious, because I know it can look impatient or rude, but yeah, I do feel kind of constantly wound up sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others' conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m barging in all the time, but I have noticed I’ll cut people off more than I should.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s talking with her parents, I might jump in before I really should. Or at work, if an employee is explaining something, I’ll kind of finish their thought because I think I know where they’re headed. I usually mean it to be helpful, but… I can tell sometimes it rubs people the wrong way. So, yeah, I probably do intrude more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still keeping up overall. I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart, because it’s not. I do my job, I show up for my family.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, it does cause some problems. At home, I think it’s starting to cost me a little trust when plans don’t go the way they were supposed to. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, people can get kind of worn down or less patient with me if I jump ahead or don’t handle the conversation the way I should.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is affecting things. Not severely, maybe, but enough that I’m noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, to the point where others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to dominate the room or anything. But I do catch myself talking a lot more than I meant to.\n\nEspecially at work, if I’m explaining a plan or walking someone through what I want done, I’ll get going and sort of keep adding details. Then I notice the other person hasn’t really gotten a chance to say much. Sometimes I can see it on their face before I realize it.\n\nSo… yes, probably. I wouldn’t say it’s constant, but it happens enough that I’m aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, to some degree. I’m still functioning, you know? I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m getting through work, I’m taking care of things at home.\n\nBut it does wear on me a little. When family plans don’t go the way they should, I can feel that trust at home take a hit. And at work, if meetings or mentoring conversations go sideways because of how I handle them, I can tell people get less patient with me.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it affects my quality of life some. Not in a huge dramatic way, but enough that I don’t feel great about it.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless as a child, compared to your peers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I was a kid, so it’s hard to know what was “normal,” but looking back, I was probably more restless than some of the other kids.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they’d even finished asking the question. I don’t know if that counts exactly, but… yeah, that was definitely a thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or following instructions as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t remember it being a big thing, at least not like… nobody sat me down and said I couldn’t pay attention or follow directions.\n\nI probably missed things here and there like any kid, but I don’t want to overstate it. The stuff I remember more clearly was being kind of restless or talking when I shouldn’t. With instructions, I think I usually managed okay if someone kept me on track. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and impulsivity as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, looking back, I guess there were some similar things. I don’t want to make it sound like I was out of control or anything, but teachers did move my seat around a lot because I’d talk too much, or get up when I probably wasn’t supposed to.\n\nAnd I remember blurting out answers before they’d even finished asking the question. So… more the impulsive, restless side, I think. If that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your symptoms are causing mild, moderate, or significant problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say mild… maybe edging toward moderate sometimes, but I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI’m still keeping up overall. Work is getting done, home life is still functioning. But it does cost me a bit, I guess. People at work can lose patience with me in meetings or mentoring conversations when they don’t go smoothly, and at home it can affect trust when family plans don’t happen the way they should.\n\nSo… I’d say mild, but noticeable. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been consistently present and problematic across multiple settings, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… across a few areas, but I’d still call it pretty manageable overall.\n\nAt work, it shows up mostly when meetings or mentoring conversations don’t go as smoothly as they should, and I can tell people get a little impatient with me. At home, it’s more like family plans not happening the way they were supposed to, and that can chip away at trust a bit.\n\nSocially, I don’t know if I’d say it’s a major problem, but the same pattern can kind of carry over. I’m still keeping up, though. It’s not like everything is falling apart. It’s just… starting to cost me in small ways, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the problems caused by your symptoms as significantly impacting your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say “significantly” like everything is falling apart… I’m still keeping up overall.\n\nBut, yeah, it is starting to have an impact. At work, people can lose patience when meetings or mentoring conversations don’t go the way they should. And at home, if family plans don’t happen like I said they would, it affects trust a bit.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it’s not severe, but it’s more than nothing. Is that a fair way to put it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People at work can lose patience with me in meetings or mentoring conversations when they don’t go smoothly, and at home it can affect trust when family plans don’t happen the way they should.\n\nSo… I’d say mild, but noticeable. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been consistently present and problematic across multiple settings, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… across a few areas, but I’d still call it pretty manageable overall.\n\nAt work, it shows up mostly when meetings or mentoring conversations don’t go as smoothly as they should, and I can tell people get a little impatient with me. At home, it’s more like family plans not happening the way they were supposed to, and that can chip away at trust a bit.\n\nSocially, I don’t know if I’d say it’s a major problem, but the same pattern can kind of carry over. I’m still keeping up, though. It’s not like everything is falling apart. It’s just… starting to cost me in small ways, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the problems caused by your symptoms as significantly impacting your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say “significantly” like everything is falling apart… I’m still keeping up overall.\n\nBut, yeah, it is starting to have an impact. At work, people can lose patience when meetings or mentoring conversations don’t go the way they should. And at home, if family plans don’t happen like I said they would, it affects trust a bit.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it’s not severe, but it’s more than nothing. Is that a fair way to put it?\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your symptoms are currently causing you more than mild problems in your daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / chief_executive / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still keeping up overall, but it’s starting to cost me trust at home and patience from people at work when meetings, mentoring conversations, or family plans don’t go the way they should.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe… a little more than mild at times, but I’d be hesitant to call it moderate exactly.

I’m still keeping up overall, you know, work gets done and things at home are basically okay. But it is starting to cost me some. At work, people can get impatient when meetings or mentoring conversations don’t go the way they should. And at home, when family plans don’t happen like they were supposed to, it affects trust a bit.

So… I guess I’d say mostly mild, but not nothing. Is that a reasonable way to answer?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I guess what finally made me come in is that over the past year or so, I’ve been getting pulled off task really easily. It’s not anything dramatic, I don’t think, but even a phone buzz, someone walking past my office, or just some random thought—like about my in-laws—can derail what I’m doing before I really catch it. I figured I should probably ask someone if that’s normal or if I’m just… not managing it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s out of control or anything, but I do notice it. In meetings especially, I’m usually bouncing my knee or clicking a pen, or I’ll keep shifting around in the chair. I’m trying to listen, honestly, but sometimes people give me these looks like I’m rushing them or impatient, and that’s not really what I mean to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I don’t mean to be rude, but I’ve noticed I’ll jump in before someone’s finished. Like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, I’ll cut in, or at work I might finish an employee’s thought before they’ve actually said it. I usually think I’m helping or moving things along, but… I can tell it rubs people the wrong way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing group activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that as a big problem, honestly. Lines can annoy me like anyone else, but I don’t think I’m cutting ahead or making a scene or anything. In group stuff, I try to wait my turn… I mean, maybe I get a little eager sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects due to disorganization or difficulty with planning?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a major problem. I mean, I run a company, so I have to plan things and get things finished. Sometimes I probably rely on other people or reminders to keep things moving, but… I don’t think I’m dropping the ball all the time or anything. Maybe if you mean personal projects at home, those can kind of sit around longer than they should, but I’ve always figured that’s pretty normal. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always, but it does happen. Like I can start a planning document or sit down with one of my managers and feel pretty locked in at first, and then after a few minutes I realize I’ve kind of missed half of what they said. Then I have to ask them to run it back, which is a little embarrassing. I try to cover it well, but yeah, focusing for a long stretch can be harder than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably do that more than I’d like to admit. I don’t usually mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll jump in before someone’s finished, like with my wife when she’s talking to her parents, or at work I’ll finish an employee’s thought because I think I know where they’re going. Sometimes I can tell afterward it didn’t land well. I try to catch myself, but… yeah, it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself quitting tasks or activities before you've finished them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I quit things a lot. I mean, if something matters, I usually get it done, especially at work. There are probably things I start and then set aside for a while, but I don’t know if that’s “quitting” exactly. Maybe more like I move on to something else and come back later… or sometimes don’t, if it wasn’t that important. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're 'on the go' or like you're constantly in motion, even when you're not necessarily supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a dramatic way, I don’t think. But when I’m supposed to just sit still, like at a family dinner or one of the church volunteer meetings, I can feel this sort of… motor running inside. Like I need to move, or tap my foot, or do something with my hands.\n\nI try not to make it obvious, because I know it can look impatient or rude, but yeah, I do feel kind of constantly wound up sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others' conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m barging in all the time, but I have noticed I’ll cut people off more than I should.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s talking with her parents, I might jump in before I really should. Or at work, if an employee is explaining something, I’ll kind of finish their thought because I think I know where they’re headed. I usually mean it to be helpful, but… I can tell sometimes it rubs people the wrong way. So, yeah, I probably do intrude more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still keeping up overall. I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart, because it’s not. I do my job, I show up for my family.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, it does cause some problems. At home, I think it’s starting to cost me a little trust when plans don’t go the way they were supposed to. And at work, in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, people can get kind of worn down or less patient with me if I jump ahead or don’t handle the conversation the way I should.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is affecting things. Not severely, maybe, but enough that I’m noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, to the point where others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m trying to dominate the room or anything. But I do catch myself talking a lot more than I meant to.\n\nEspecially at work, if I’m explaining a plan or walking someone through what I want done, I’ll get going and sort of keep adding details. Then I notice the other person hasn’t really gotten a chance to say much. Sometimes I can see it on their face before I realize it.\n\nSo… yes, probably. I wouldn’t say it’s constant, but it happens enough that I’m aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties are affecting your overall well-being or quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, to some degree. I’m still functioning, you know? I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m getting through work, I’m taking care of things at home.\n\nBut it does wear on me a little. When family plans don’t go the way they should, I can feel that trust at home take a hit. And at work, if meetings or mentoring conversations go sideways because of how I handle them, I can tell people get less patient with me.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it affects my quality of life some. Not in a huge dramatic way, but enough that I don’t feel great about it.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless as a child, compared to your peers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I was a kid, so it’s hard to know what was “normal,” but looking back, I was probably more restless than some of the other kids.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d answer before they’d even finished asking the question. I don’t know if that counts exactly, but… yeah, that was definitely a thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or following instructions as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t remember it being a big thing, at least not like… nobody sat me down and said I couldn’t pay attention or follow directions.\n\nI probably missed things here and there like any kid, but I don’t want to overstate it. The stuff I remember more clearly was being kind of restless or talking when I shouldn’t. With instructions, I think I usually managed okay if someone kept me on track. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and impulsivity as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, looking back, I guess there were some similar things. I don’t want to make it sound like I was out of control or anything, but teachers did move my seat around a lot because I’d talk too much, or get up when I probably wasn’t supposed to.\n\nAnd I remember blurting out answers before they’d even finished asking the question. So… more the impulsive, restless side, I think.
